Um, mainly work. I’m a registered nurse, and on the floor I keep losing my train of thought constantly. I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing.

Or I’ll set a reminder to pay a bill and still somehow not actually do it—like I see it, swipe it away because I’m “about to,” and then it’s gone from my brain.\n\nAppointments too. I’ve shown up late or almost missed them even when I knew they were coming. I fully mean to do it, and then it just disappears unless something puts it directly back in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Um… impulsive how, exactly? Like, I don’t think I’m reckless in a big dramatic way. I’m not, like, randomly quitting jobs or buying a motorcycle or something.

And online shopping is its own trap—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I was looking at compression socks the other day and somehow ended up reading reviews for meal prep containers for like twenty minutes.

So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed “acting on impulse” as a main issue, I guess. What kind of things do you mean?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… foggy is probably a good word for it, but it’s not always like I’m sleepy or something. It’s more like my thought just gets knocked sideways. The main reason I’m here is that on the floor I lose my train of thought constantly.

Like I’ll be in the middle of figuring out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby—not even me, sometimes—and suddenly I’m standing there like, wait, what was I just doing? And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.

But even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.

Yeah, I do. I get really anxious, honestly, because it’s usually stuff I fully intended to do. Like calling a family member back, or following up on a missing order, and then later I realize it never happened and my stomach just drops.

I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I get really anxious, honestly, because it’s usually stuff I fully intended to do. Like calling a family member back, or following up on a missing order, and then later I realize it never happened and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. At home I’ll forget to pay a bill or I’ll miss an appointment even though I meant to go—sometimes even if I put a reminder in my phone, which is… embarrassing. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. But yes, when I realize I forgot something routine, I feel overwhelmed and kind of panicky.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your difficulties with focus, memory, and organization are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 12\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… I mean, with family and friends I’m sure it does some, but the place it’s really showing up is work. Sorry, I know you asked relationships—my brain goes straight to work because that’s where it’s become… like, official, I guess.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, mainly work. I’m a registered nurse, and on the floor I keep losing my train of thought constantly. Like I’ll be trying to sort out some patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and suddenly it’s just… gone. I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing.\n\nAnd I know hospitals are always noisy—like, that’s the job, right? The pumps never stop, and half the time it’s the same alarm tone for something totally different, which drives me crazy—but it feels like I’m getting pulled off track by every little thing lately. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing difficulties with focus or getting distracted in situations outside of your nursing work?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I’m not totally sure how to separate it, because the floor is where it’s the most obvious. Like, that’s really why I came in—when I’m trying to deal with some patient flow thing and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse something, or a call light goes off, my thought just disappears.\n\nI know that’s still “work,” sorry—that’s the example I keep coming back to because it happens constantly there. It’s like the noise or interruption yanks me off whatever I was doing, and then I’m standing there trying to reconstruct it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or having trouble focusing when you're in a quiet place, like at home or in a library?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… quiet places, I guess I don’t notice it as much? The thing that’s really making me worried is the floor. Like I’ll be in the middle of trying to figure out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just gets wiped out. \n\nSorry, I know you asked about home or a library. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it’s constant, and it’s the reason I finally came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened a few days ago?\n\nPatient: Um… a few days ago? I don’t know, I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that specifically. Like, if you mean whole conversations or events, I’d have to think about it.\n\nSometimes the days sort of run together, but with nursing shifts that happens anyway—like you’ll swear something happened Tuesday and it was actually Sunday, because the schedule is weird and the unit is just… its own little time zone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So, I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things like appointments, tasks, or instructions outside of your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, outside of work it happens too, which is embarrassing because some of it is such normal adult stuff. Like I’ll mean to call my sister back, and then three days later I realize I never did. Or I’ll set a reminder to pay a bill and still somehow not actually do it—like I see it, swipe it away because I’m “about to,” and then it’s gone from my brain.\n\nAppointments too. I’ve shown up late or almost missed them even when I knew they were coming. And instructions… I’ll have the intention, like, okay, follow up on that missing order—sorry, that’s work again—but it’s the same feeling. I fully mean to do it, and then it just disappears unless something puts it directly back in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as, like, bouncing off the walls or anything. I mean, at work I’m moving constantly because that’s the job, so it’s hard to tell what’s “restless” versus just… nursing.\n\nAt home, maybe I shift around or get up to do something and then—I don’t know, I’m thinking of how my couch has this one cushion that sinks in and it drives me crazy, so I keep changing positions. But I’m not sure that’s what you mean by fidgety. Could you say what you mean a little more?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… impulsive how, exactly? Like, I don’t think I’m reckless in a big dramatic way. I’m not, like, randomly quitting jobs or buying a motorcycle or something.\n\nI mean, I guess everybody does small things without thinking, right? Like I’ll say something and then later wonder if it sounded weird, but that might just be me overthinking it after. And online shopping is its own trap—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I was looking at compression socks the other day and somehow ended up reading reviews for meal prep containers for like twenty minutes.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed “acting on impulse” as a main issue, I guess. What kind of things do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy, making it hard to think clearly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… foggy is probably a good word for it, but it’s not always like I’m sleepy or something. It’s more like my thought just gets knocked sideways. The main reason I’m here is that on the floor I lose my train of thought constantly.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of figuring out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby—not even me, sometimes—and suddenly I’m standing there like, wait, what was I just doing? And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much as at work, I think. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So, I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things like appointments, tasks, or instructions outside of your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, outside of work it happens too, which is embarrassing because some of it is such normal adult stuff. Like I’ll mean to call my sister back, and then three days later I realize I never did. Or I’ll set a reminder to pay a bill and still somehow not actually do it—like I see it, swipe it away because I’m “about to,” and then it’s gone from my brain.\n\nAppointments too. I’ve shown up late or almost missed them even when I knew they were coming. And instructions… I’ll have the intention, like, okay, follow up on that missing order—sorry, that’s work again—but it’s the same feeling. I fully mean to do it, and then it just disappears unless something puts it directly back in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as, like, bouncing off the walls or anything. I mean, at work I’m moving constantly because that’s the job, so it’s hard to tell what’s “restless” versus just… nursing.\n\nAt home, maybe I shift around or get up to do something and then—I don’t know, I’m thinking of how my couch has this one cushion that sinks in and it drives me crazy, so I keep changing positions. But I’m not sure that’s what you mean by fidgety. Could you say what you mean a little more?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… impulsive how, exactly? Like, I don’t think I’m reckless in a big dramatic way. I’m not, like, randomly quitting jobs or buying a motorcycle or something.\n\nI mean, I guess everybody does small things without thinking, right? Like I’ll say something and then later wonder if it sounded weird, but that might just be me overthinking it after. And online shopping is its own trap—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I was looking at compression socks the other day and somehow ended up reading reviews for meal prep containers for like twenty minutes.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed “acting on impulse” as a main issue, I guess. What kind of things do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy, making it hard to think clearly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… foggy is probably a good word for it, but it’s not always like I’m sleepy or something. It’s more like my thought just gets knocked sideways. The main reason I’m here is that on the floor I lose my train of thought constantly.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of figuring out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby—not even me, sometimes—and suddenly I’m standing there like, wait, what was I just doing? And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much as at work, I think. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much as at work, I think. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like calling a family member back, or following up on a missing order, and then later I realize it never happened and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. At home I’ll forget to pay a bill or I’ll miss an appointment even though I meant to go—sometimes even if I put a reminder in my phone, which is… embarrassing. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. But yes, when I realize I forgot something routine, I feel overwhelmed and kind of panicky.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, memory, and organization are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family and friends I’m sure it does some, but the place it’s really showing up is work. Sorry, I know you asked relationships—my brain goes straight to work because that’s where it’s become… like, official, I guess.\n\nI’m on formal supervision now, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. That feels awful. I’m supposed to be the person helping keep things moving, and instead I feel like people are watching to make sure I didn’t miss something. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I still get through the day, but it feels like I’m barely holding it together sometimes. And then I start thinking about work, and—sorry, this is where my mind keeps going—but that’s the part that really affects me.\n\nThe concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check pieces of my follow-through. I was looking at compression socks the other day and somehow ended up reading reviews for meal prep containers for like twenty minutes.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed “acting on impulse” as a main issue, I guess. What kind of things do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy, making it hard to think clearly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… foggy is probably a good word for it, but it’s not always like I’m sleepy or something. It’s more like my thought just gets knocked sideways. The main reason I’m here is that on the floor I lose my train of thought constantly.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of figuring out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby—not even me, sometimes—and suddenly I’m standing there like, wait, what was I just doing? And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much as at work, I think. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like calling a family member back, or following up on a missing order, and then later I realize it never happened and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. That feels awful. I’m supposed to be the person helping keep things moving, and instead I feel like people are watching to make sure I didn’t miss something. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. And I know that’s there for safety, I get it, but it makes me feel awful. Like I’m supposed to be the one leading and keeping things organized, and instead I feel like I’m turning into a liability. That really weighs on me day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you think about your difficulties with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I get really frustrated with myself, and embarrassed, and then I kind of spiral into thinking about all the ways I should be doing better. Like, even small things make me think of a whole chain of other things I’ve messed up, and then I’m not even thinking about the original thing anymore.\n\nSo… sad, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I get really anxious, honestly, because it’s usually stuff I fully intended to do. Like calling a family member back, or following up on a missing order, and then later I realize it never happened and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. At home I’ll forget to pay a bill or I’ll miss an appointment even though I meant to go—sometimes even if I put a reminder in my phone, which is… embarrassing. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. But yes, when I realize I forgot something routine, I feel overwhelmed and kind of panicky.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, memory, and organization are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family and friends I’m sure it does some, but the place it’s really showing up is work. Sorry, I know you asked relationships—my brain goes straight to work because that’s where it’s become… like, official, I guess.\n\nI’m on formal supervision now, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. That feels awful. I’m supposed to be the person helping keep things moving, and instead I feel like people are watching to make sure I didn’t miss something. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I still get through the day, but it feels like I’m barely holding it together sometimes. And then I start thinking about work, and—sorry, this is where my mind keeps going—but that’s the part that really affects me.\n\nThe concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check pieces of my follow-through. And I know that’s there for safety, I get it, but it makes me feel awful. Like I’m supposed to be the one leading and keeping things organized, and instead I feel like I’m turning into a liability. That really weighs on me day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you think about your difficulties with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I get really frustrated with myself, and embarrassed, and then I kind of spiral into thinking about all the ways I should be doing better. Like, even small things make me think of a whole chain of other things I’ve messed up, and then I’m not even thinking about the original thing anymore.\n\nSo… sad, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word. It’s more like disappointment in myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or upset when you're unable to focus or remember things?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate anxious from just really irritated with myself. Like when I can’t remember what I was about to do, I’ll get upset, but then I start thinking, “Okay, why did I even walk over here?” and then I notice something else that needs doing, and suddenly I’m annoyed about that too.\n\nSo… yes, upset sometimes, I guess. Anxious maybe? I don’t know. It’s more like my brain gets loud and I get frustrated, and then it’s hard to just calmly go back to what I was trying to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think significant is probably the right word, even if I don’t always call it that in the moment. It’s not just “oh, I forgot something.” At work it’s gotten to the point where I’m on formal supervision, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through.

And I know that’s important for patient safety—like, I completely understand why it has to happen—but emotionally it really gets to me. I’m supposed to be the person leading and keeping things moving, and instead I feel like I’m becoming a liability. That’s… that’s really hard to sit with.

And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely outside of work too. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there are other people depending on me and there’s documentation and follow-up and all of that, but at home it’s… it’s the same pattern, just with different things.

Like I’ll mean to call a family member back, and I genuinely intend to do it, and then suddenly it’s two days later. Or I’ll forget to pay a bill even after I put a reminder in my phone, which is embarrassing because then I’ll see the reminder later and think, “How did I still not do that?”

And it happens with appointments too. I don’t want to make it sound like I don’t care, because I do care. I’ll plan to show up, I’ll have it in my head, and then somehow it just slips. It’s not only at work. Work is just where the consequences feel louder.

It’s more like my thought just gets knocked sideways. The main reason I’m here is that on the floor I lose my train of thought constantly.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of figuring out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby—not even me, sometimes—and suddenly I’m standing there like, wait, what was I just doing? And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much as at work, I think. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I get really anxious, honestly, because it’s usually stuff I fully intended to do. At home I’ll forget to pay a bill or I’ll miss an appointment even though I meant to go—sometimes even if I put a reminder in my phone, which is… embarrassing. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. But yes, when I realize I forgot something routine, I feel overwhelmed and kind of panicky.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, memory, and organization are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family and friends I’m sure it does some, but the place it’s really showing up is work. That feels awful. I’m supposed to be the person helping keep things moving, and instead I feel like people are watching to make sure I didn’t miss something. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. The main reason I’m here is that on the floor I lose my train of thought constantly.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of figuring out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby—not even me, sometimes—and suddenly I’m standing there like, wait, what was I just doing? And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much as at work, I think. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I get really anxious, honestly, because it’s usually stuff I fully intended to do. Like calling a family member back, or following up on a missing order, and then later I realize it never happened and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. At home I’ll forget to pay a bill or I’ll miss an appointment even though I meant to go—sometimes even if I put a reminder in my phone, which is… embarrassing. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. But yes, when I realize I forgot something routine, I feel overwhelmed and kind of panicky.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, memory, and organization are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family and friends I’m sure it does some, but the place it’s really showing up is work. Sorry, I know you asked relationships—my brain goes straight to work because that’s where it’s become… like, official, I guess.\n\nI’m on formal supervision now, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. That feels awful. I’m supposed to be the person helping keep things moving, and instead I feel like people are watching to make sure I didn’t miss something. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I still get through the day, but it feels like I’m barely holding it together sometimes. And then I start thinking about work, and—sorry, this is where my mind keeps going—but that’s the part that really affects me.\n\nThe concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check pieces of my follow-through. And I know that’s there for safety, I get it, but it makes me feel awful. Like I’m supposed to be the one leading and keeping things organized, and instead I feel like I’m turning into a liability. That really weighs on me day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you think about your difficulties with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. Like, even small things make me think of a whole chain of other things I’ve messed up, and then I’m not even thinking about the original thing anymore.\n\nSo… sad, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word. At home I’ll forget to pay a bill or I’ll miss an appointment even though I meant to go—sometimes even if I put a reminder in my phone, which is… embarrassing. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. But yes, when I realize I forgot something routine, I feel overwhelmed and kind of panicky.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, memory, and organization are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family and friends I’m sure it does some, but the place it’s really showing up is work. Sorry, I know you asked relationships—my brain goes straight to work because that’s where it’s become… like, official, I guess.\n\nI’m on formal supervision now, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. That feels awful. I’m supposed to be the person helping keep things moving, and instead I feel like people are watching to make sure I didn’t miss something. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I still get through the day, but it feels like I’m barely holding it together sometimes. And then I start thinking about work, and—sorry, this is where my mind keeps going—but that’s the part that really affects me.\n\nThe concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check pieces of my follow-through. And I know that’s there for safety, I get it, but it makes me feel awful. Like I’m supposed to be the one leading and keeping things organized, and instead I feel like I’m turning into a liability. That really weighs on me day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you think about your difficulties with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I get really frustrated with myself, and embarrassed, and then I kind of spiral into thinking about all the ways I should be doing better. Like, even small things make me think of a whole chain of other things I’ve messed up, and then I’m not even thinking about the original thing anymore.\n\nSo… sad, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word. It’s more like disappointment in myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or upset when you're unable to focus or remember things?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate anxious from just really irritated with myself. Like when I can’t remember what I was about to do, I’ll get upset, but then I start thinking, “Okay, why did I even walk over here?” and then I notice something else that needs doing, and suddenly I’m annoyed about that too.\n\nSo… yes, upset sometimes, I guess. Anxious maybe? I don’t know. It’s more like my brain gets loud and I get frustrated, and then it’s hard to just calmly go back to what I was trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and memory are causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think significant is probably the right word, even if I don’t always call it that in the moment. It’s not just “oh, I forgot something.” At work it’s gotten to the point where I’m on formal supervision, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through.\n\nAnd I know that’s important for patient safety—like, I completely understand why it has to happen—but emotionally it really gets to me. I’m supposed to be the person leading and keeping things moving, and instead I feel like I’m becoming a liability. That’s… that’s really hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and memory at home or in other settings outside of work as frequently as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely outside of work too. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there are other people depending on me and there’s documentation and follow-up and all of that, but at home it’s… it’s the same pattern, just with different things.\n\nLike I’ll mean to call a family member back, and I genuinely intend to do it, and then suddenly it’s two days later. Or I’ll forget to pay a bill even after I put a reminder in my phone, which is embarrassing because then I’ll see the reminder later and think, “How did I still not do that?”\n\nAnd it happens with appointments too. I don’t want to make it sound like I don’t care, because I do care. I’ll plan to show up, I’ll have it in my head, and then somehow it just slips. It’s not only at work. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there are other people depending on me and there’s documentation and follow-up and all of that, but at home it’s… it’s the same pattern, just with different things.\n\nLike I’ll mean to call a family member back, and I genuinely intend to do it, and then suddenly it’s two days later. Or I’ll forget to pay a bill even after I put a reminder in my phone, which is embarrassing because then I’ll see the reminder later and think, “How did I still not do that?”\n\nAnd it happens with appointments too. I don’t want to make it sound like I don’t care, because I do care. I’ll plan to show up, I’ll have it in my head, and then somehow it just slips. It’s not only at work. Work is just where the consequences feel louder.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your daily routines or responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I mean, it’s embarrassing because these are basic adult things, you know? Like calling someone back in my family—I’ll fully mean to, and then I’ll remember at some random time when I can’t do it, and then forget again.\n\nBills too. I’ll even set a reminder sometimes, and then somehow I still don’t actually pay it when I’m supposed to. Or appointments—I have every intention of going, and then it just slips away from me until it’s too late.\n\nIt’s kind of the same thing that happens at work, like with following up on an order that’s missing. I’ll think, “Okay, I need to circle back to that,” and then something else pulls my attention, and it’s gone. So yeah, it definitely affects my routines and responsibilities at home, not just work.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your relationships with your family or friends outside of work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / registered_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"I forget things that should be routine, like calling a family member back, following up on a missing order, paying a bill at home, or showing up for an appointment, even when I meant to do it and sometimes even after I set a reminder.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it does. I mean, not like constant fighting or anything, but it affects them because people feel ignored, I think. Like I’ll mean to call a family member back—actually mean to—and then I remember while I’m driving or in the shower or in the middle of something, and then by the time I can do it, it’s gone again.

And then it’s two days later and I feel awful, because from their side it probably looks like I just didn’t care enough to call. It reminds me of work stuff too, like when I need to follow up on a missing order and I tell myself, “Okay, come back to that,” and then something else happens and I lose it.

At home it’s bills or appointments too, even with reminders sometimes, which isn’t exactly a friendship thing, but it all kind of makes me seem unreliable. And I hate that, because I’m not trying to be flaky. I really do mean to follow through.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, mainly work. I’m a registered nurse, and on the floor I keep losing my train of thought constantly. Like I’ll be trying to sort out some patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and suddenly it’s just… gone. I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing.\n\nAnd I know hospitals are always noisy—like, that’s the job, right? The pumps never stop, and half the time it’s the same alarm tone for something totally different, which drives me crazy—but it feels like I’m getting pulled off track by every little thing lately. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing difficulties with focus or getting distracted in situations outside of your nursing work?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I’m not totally sure how to separate it, because the floor is where it’s the most obvious. Like, that’s really why I came in—when I’m trying to deal with some patient flow thing and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse something, or a call light goes off, my thought just disappears.\n\nI know that’s still “work,” sorry—that’s the example I keep coming back to because it happens constantly there. It’s like the noise or interruption yanks me off whatever I was doing, and then I’m standing there trying to reconstruct it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or having trouble focusing when you're in a quiet place, like at home or in a library?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… quiet places, I guess I don’t notice it as much? The thing that’s really making me worried is the floor. Like I’ll be in the middle of trying to figure out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just gets wiped out. \n\nSorry, I know you asked about home or a library. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it’s constant, and it’s the reason I finally came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened a few days ago?\n\nPatient: Um… a few days ago? I don’t know, I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that specifically. Like, if you mean whole conversations or events, I’d have to think about it.\n\nSometimes the days sort of run together, but with nursing shifts that happens anyway—like you’ll swear something happened Tuesday and it was actually Sunday, because the schedule is weird and the unit is just… its own little time zone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So, I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things like appointments, tasks, or instructions outside of your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, outside of work it happens too, which is embarrassing because some of it is such normal adult stuff. Like I’ll mean to call my sister back, and then three days later I realize I never did. Or I’ll set a reminder to pay a bill and still somehow not actually do it—like I see it, swipe it away because I’m “about to,” and then it’s gone from my brain.\n\nAppointments too. I’ve shown up late or almost missed them even when I knew they were coming. And instructions… I’ll have the intention, like, okay, follow up on that missing order—sorry, that’s work again—but it’s the same feeling. I fully mean to do it, and then it just disappears unless something puts it directly back in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as, like, bouncing off the walls or anything. I mean, at work I’m moving constantly because that’s the job, so it’s hard to tell what’s “restless” versus just… nursing.\n\nAt home, maybe I shift around or get up to do something and then—I don’t know, I’m thinking of how my couch has this one cushion that sinks in and it drives me crazy, so I keep changing positions. But I’m not sure that’s what you mean by fidgety. Could you say what you mean a little more?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… impulsive how, exactly? Like, I don’t think I’m reckless in a big dramatic way. I’m not, like, randomly quitting jobs or buying a motorcycle or something.\n\nI mean, I guess everybody does small things without thinking, right? Like I’ll say something and then later wonder if it sounded weird, but that might just be me overthinking it after. And online shopping is its own trap—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I was looking at compression socks the other day and somehow ended up reading reviews for meal prep containers for like twenty minutes.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed “acting on impulse” as a main issue, I guess. What kind of things do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy, making it hard to think clearly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… foggy is probably a good word for it, but it’s not always like I’m sleepy or something. It’s more like my thought just gets knocked sideways. The main reason I’m here is that on the floor I lose my train of thought constantly.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of figuring out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby—not even me, sometimes—and suddenly I’m standing there like, wait, what was I just doing? And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much as at work, I think. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. The main place I notice the foggy, cloudy feeling is definitely work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is pretty rough, especially when I’m charge nurse. I’ll try to make a system, like I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes on the desk, a half-started list on the report sheet—sometimes I rewrite the list because it looks too messy, which is ridiculous because then I’ve wasted more time.\n\nBut even with all that, I still can’t always figure out what needs to happen first. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I get really anxious, honestly, because it’s usually stuff I fully intended to do. Like calling a family member back, or following up on a missing order, and then later I realize it never happened and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. At home I’ll forget to pay a bill or I’ll miss an appointment even though I meant to go—sometimes even if I put a reminder in my phone, which is… embarrassing. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. But yes, when I realize I forgot something routine, I feel overwhelmed and kind of panicky.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, memory, and organization are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family and friends I’m sure it does some, but the place it’s really showing up is work. Sorry, I know you asked relationships—my brain goes straight to work because that’s where it’s become… like, official, I guess.\n\nI’m on formal supervision now, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. That feels awful. I’m supposed to be the person helping keep things moving, and instead I feel like people are watching to make sure I didn’t miss something. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I still get through the day, but it feels like I’m barely holding it together sometimes. And then I start thinking about work, and—sorry, this is where my mind keeps going—but that’s the part that really affects me.\n\nThe concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check pieces of my follow-through. And I know that’s there for safety, I get it, but it makes me feel awful. Like I’m supposed to be the one leading and keeping things organized, and instead I feel like I’m turning into a liability. That really weighs on me day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you think about your difficulties with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I get really frustrated with myself, and embarrassed, and then I kind of spiral into thinking about all the ways I should be doing better. Like, even small things make me think of a whole chain of other things I’ve messed up, and then I’m not even thinking about the original thing anymore.\n\nSo… sad, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word. It’s more like disappointment in myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or upset when you're unable to focus or remember things?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate anxious from just really irritated with myself. Like when I can’t remember what I was about to do, I’ll get upset, but then I start thinking, “Okay, why did I even walk over here?” and then I notice something else that needs doing, and suddenly I’m annoyed about that too.\n\nSo… yes, upset sometimes, I guess. Anxious maybe? I don’t know. It’s more like my brain gets loud and I get frustrated, and then it’s hard to just calmly go back to what I was trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and memory are causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think significant is probably the right word, even if I don’t always call it that in the moment. It’s not just “oh, I forgot something.” At work it’s gotten to the point where I’m on formal supervision, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through.\n\nAnd I know that’s important for patient safety—like, I completely understand why it has to happen—but emotionally it really gets to me. I’m supposed to be the person leading and keeping things moving, and instead I feel like I’m becoming a liability. That’s… that’s really hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and memory at home or in other settings outside of work as frequently as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely outside of work too. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there are other people depending on me and there’s documentation and follow-up and all of that, but at home it’s… it’s the same pattern, just with different things.\n\nLike I’ll mean to call a family member back, and I genuinely intend to do it, and then suddenly it’s two days later. Or I’ll forget to pay a bill even after I put a reminder in my phone, which is embarrassing because then I’ll see the reminder later and think, “How did I still not do that?”\n\nAnd it happens with appointments too. I don’t want to make it sound like I don’t care, because I do care. I’ll plan to show up, I’ll have it in my head, and then somehow it just slips. It’s not only at work. Work is just where the consequences feel louder.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your daily routines or responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I mean, I still get through the day, but it feels like I’m barely holding it together sometimes. And then I start thinking about work, and—sorry, this is where my mind keeps going—but that’s the part that really affects me.\n\nThe concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check pieces of my follow-through. And I know that’s there for safety, I get it, but it makes me feel awful. Like I’m supposed to be the one leading and keeping things organized, and instead I feel like I’m turning into a liability. That really weighs on me day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you think about your difficulties with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I get really frustrated with myself, and embarrassed, and then I kind of spiral into thinking about all the ways I should be doing better. Like, even small things make me think of a whole chain of other things I’ve messed up, and then I’m not even thinking about the original thing anymore.\n\nSo… sad, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word. It’s more like disappointment in myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or upset when you're unable to focus or remember things?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate anxious from just really irritated with myself. Like when I can’t remember what I was about to do, I’ll get upset, but then I start thinking, “Okay, why did I even walk over here?” and then I notice something else that needs doing, and suddenly I’m annoyed about that too.\n\nSo… yes, upset sometimes, I guess. Anxious maybe? I don’t know. It’s more like my brain gets loud and I get frustrated, and then it’s hard to just calmly go back to what I was trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and memory are causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think significant is probably the right word, even if I don’t always call it that in the moment. It’s not just “oh, I forgot something.” At work it’s gotten to the point where I’m on formal supervision, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through.\n\nAnd I know that’s important for patient safety—like, I completely understand why it has to happen—but emotionally it really gets to me. I’m supposed to be the person leading and keeping things moving, and instead I feel like I’m becoming a liability. That’s… that’s really hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and memory at home or in other settings outside of work as frequently as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely outside of work too. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there are other people depending on me and there’s documentation and follow-up and all of that, but at home it’s… it’s the same pattern, just with different things.\n\nLike I’ll mean to call a family member back, and I genuinely intend to do it, and then suddenly it’s two days later. Or I’ll forget to pay a bill even after I put a reminder in my phone, which is embarrassing because then I’ll see the reminder later and think, “How did I still not do that?”\n\nAnd it happens with appointments too. I don’t want to make it sound like I don’t care, because I do care. I’ll plan to show up, I’ll have it in my head, and then somehow it just slips. It’s not only at work. Work is just where the consequences feel louder.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your daily routines or responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ll even set a reminder sometimes, and then somehow I still don’t actually pay it when I’m supposed to. Or appointments—I have every intention of going, and then it just slips away from me until it’s too late.\n\nIt’s kind of the same thing that happens at work, like with following up on an order that’s missing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the paying attention part… or, I guess, getting pulled off attention. Because it’s not like the information is totally gone from my brain forever. It’s more that I lose the thread.

Like the main reason I’m here is what happens on the floor. I’ll be working through a patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’m just… not on the thing anymore. And then I’m standing there trying to remember, “Wait, what was I just doing?”

So it feels less like I can’t remember anything at all, and more like my attention gets yanked away so fast that the thought doesn’t stay long enough for me to finish it.

And then I’m trying to backtrack in my head, which is hard because the floor is never quiet. It feels cloudy in that I can’t hold the thread unless everything stays perfectly still, which obviously it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind is foggy or cloudy when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much as at work, I think. I mean, at home it can happen, but the big, obvious version is really on the floor because there’s just constant interruption.\n\nLike I’ll be working out a patient flow thing in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s like the thought just disappears. Then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was even in the middle of.\n\nAt home it’s harder to separate because, you know, I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I’m there, but then I’m also noticing the dishes or the dog bowl or whatever—sorry, I’m wandering. So I end up just responding to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment, and then near the end of the shift I’m suddenly rushing because something important didn’t get handled earlier. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to be organized, it just doesn’t turn into an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're unable to complete tasks or remember things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I get really anxious, honestly, because it’s usually stuff I fully intended to do. Like calling a family member back, or following up on a missing order, and then later I realize it never happened and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. At home I’ll forget to pay a bill or I’ll miss an appointment even though I meant to go—sometimes even if I put a reminder in my phone, which is… embarrassing. I’ll see the reminder, think “okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow it’s gone again because I’m doing something else, or I put my phone down, or—sorry, this is exactly what happens. But yes, when I realize I forgot something routine, I feel overwhelmed and kind of panicky.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, memory, and organization are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family and friends I’m sure it does some, but the place it’s really showing up is work. Sorry, I know you asked relationships—my brain goes straight to work because that’s where it’s become… like, official, I guess.\n\nI’m on formal supervision now, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. That feels awful. I’m supposed to be the person helping keep things moving, and instead I feel like people are watching to make sure I didn’t miss something. It makes me feel less like a leader and more like I’m becoming a liability.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I still get through the day, but it feels like I’m barely holding it together sometimes. And then I start thinking about work, and—sorry, this is where my mind keeps going—but that’s the part that really affects me.\n\nThe concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check pieces of my follow-through. And I know that’s there for safety, I get it, but it makes me feel awful. Like I’m supposed to be the one leading and keeping things organized, and instead I feel like I’m turning into a liability. That really weighs on me day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you think about your difficulties with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I get really frustrated with myself, and embarrassed, and then I kind of spiral into thinking about all the ways I should be doing better. Like, even small things make me think of a whole chain of other things I’ve messed up, and then I’m not even thinking about the original thing anymore.\n\nSo… sad, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word. It’s more like disappointment in myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or upset when you're unable to focus or remember things?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate anxious from just really irritated with myself. Like when I can’t remember what I was about to do, I’ll get upset, but then I start thinking, “Okay, why did I even walk over here?” and then I notice something else that needs doing, and suddenly I’m annoyed about that too.\n\nSo… yes, upset sometimes, I guess. Anxious maybe? I don’t know. It’s more like my brain gets loud and I get frustrated, and then it’s hard to just calmly go back to what I was trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and memory are causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think significant is probably the right word, even if I don’t always call it that in the moment. It’s not just “oh, I forgot something.” At work it’s gotten to the point where I’m on formal supervision, and when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through.\n\nAnd I know that’s important for patient safety—like, I completely understand why it has to happen—but emotionally it really gets to me. I’m supposed to be the person leading and keeping things moving, and instead I feel like I’m becoming a liability. That’s… that’s really hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and memory at home or in other settings outside of work as frequently as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely outside of work too. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there are other people depending on me and there’s documentation and follow-up and all of that, but at home it’s… it’s the same pattern, just with different things.\n\nLike I’ll mean to call a family member back, and I genuinely intend to do it, and then suddenly it’s two days later. Or I’ll forget to pay a bill even after I put a reminder in my phone, which is embarrassing because then I’ll see the reminder later and think, “How did I still not do that?”\n\nAnd it happens with appointments too. I don’t want to make it sound like I don’t care, because I do care. I’ll plan to show up, I’ll have it in my head, and then somehow it just slips. It’s not only at work. Work is just where the consequences feel louder.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your daily routines or responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I mean, it’s embarrassing because these are basic adult things, you know? Like calling someone back in my family—I’ll fully mean to, and then I’ll remember at some random time when I can’t do it, and then forget again.\n\nBills too. I’ll even set a reminder sometimes, and then somehow I still don’t actually pay it when I’m supposed to. Or appointments—I have every intention of going, and then it just slips away from me until it’s too late.\n\nIt’s kind of the same thing that happens at work, like with following up on an order that’s missing. I’ll think, “Okay, I need to circle back to that,” and then something else pulls my attention, and it’s gone. So yeah, it definitely affects my routines and responsibilities at home, not just work.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your relationships with your family or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, not like constant fighting or anything, but it affects them because people feel ignored, I think. Like I’ll mean to call a family member back—actually mean to—and then I remember while I’m driving or in the shower or in the middle of something, and then by the time I can do it, it’s gone again.\n\nAnd then it’s two days later and I feel awful, because from their side it probably looks like I just didn’t care enough to call. It reminds me of work stuff too, like when I need to follow up on a missing order and I tell myself, “Okay, come back to that,” and then something else happens and I lose it.\n\nAt home it’s bills or appointments too, even with reminders sometimes, which isn’t exactly a friendship thing, but it all kind of makes me seem unreliable. And I hate that, because I’m not trying to be flaky. I really do mean to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and memory are more because you have trouble paying attention or because you genuinely can't remember things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention part… or, I guess, getting pulled off attention. Because it’s not like the information is totally gone from my brain forever. It’s more that I lose the thread.\n\nLike the main reason I’m here is what happens on the floor. I’ll be working through a patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’m just… not on the thing anymore. And then I’m standing there trying to remember, “Wait, what was I just doing?”\n\nSo it feels less like I can’t remember anything at all, and more like my attention gets yanked away so fast that the thought doesn’t stay long enough for me to finish it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
